  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2, F41.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally made me come in is this habit I’ve noticed where I just can’t seem to stay seated when everyone else is. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I’ve started to feel embarrassed by it.\n\nFor example, in meetings, I’ll suddenly get up to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room for no real reason. And at dinner, when everyone’s still sitting and talking, I’ll pop up to get something from the kitchen or fuss with something nearby, even if it’s not actually urgent. It’s like I tell myself I’m being useful, but honestly, a lot of the time I think I just feel this need to move. I’m a teacher, so I’m used to being on my feet, but it’s starting to stand out in situations where I’m really supposed to just sit still with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you're forced to sit still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s exactly the feeling, though I don’t always recognize it as anxiety in the moment. It’s more like this uncomfortable restlessness, like my body has decided it’s time to do something even if there isn’t really anything that needs doing.\n\nIn meetings, for instance, I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’ll think, “Oh, I should refill my coffee,” or “Maybe I should go check that handout at the back,” and before I’ve really considered whether it’s necessary, I’m already standing up. And it’s the same at dinner sometimes—everyone else is still sitting and talking, and I’ll get up to go to the kitchen or adjust something nearby, even though, honestly, it could wait. I sort of make an excuse for it in my head, like I’m being helpful or practical, but underneath it I think I just feel uncomfortable staying seated for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Hmm… recurring thoughts that I can’t get out of my head? I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no.\n\nI mean, I do think about things, of course—school things, conversations, little practical things I need to do—but I don’t think I have one particular thought that gets stuck and keeps coming back in a way that feels intrusive or impossible to shake. It’s more that my mind may move on to the next thing quickly, but not that I’m trapped with the same thought over and over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this restlessness happens at any particular time of day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear time-of-day pattern, honestly. It’s not like I could say, “Oh, it always happens in the morning,” or “It’s definitely worse after lunch.” I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nIt seems more tied to the situation than the clock, if that makes sense. Like, if I’m expected to sit still for a while—meetings, meals, that sort of thing—that’s when I become aware of it. But whether that’s at ten in the morning or in the evening, I don’t know that it makes a huge difference. I suppose if I’m tired or if something feels especially drawn out, I might notice it more, but I can’t confidently say there’s a particular time of day when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these things, like getting up and moving around, because you feel like you have to, rather than just because you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “have to” is closer to it than “want to,” although it’s not like a clear thought in my head saying, “You must stand up now.” It’s more subtle than that.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep doing this in situations where everyone else is still seated—meetings, dinner, that kind of thing. I’ll suddenly feel like I need to refill my coffee, or check something at the back of the room, or look at a handout, or just move over there for some reason. And in the moment it feels like it makes sense, like, “Well, I may as well do that now,” but if I look back on it, nothing was actually urgent. The coffee could wait. The handout could wait. I didn’t really need to get up right then.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it feels more like an urge or pressure to move than a simple preference. I don’t necessarily want to be the person who keeps standing up when everyone else is settled. In fact, I’d rather not draw attention to myself that way. But I’ll sort of invent a practical reason, or latch onto one, and then I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around helps you avoid any specific thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of, you know, trying to get away from a particular thought or feeling.\n\nI’ve wondered about that, actually, because when you ask it that way, it makes me think, “Am I avoiding something and just not realizing it?” But I can’t point to anything specific. It’s not usually that I’m upset, or anxious about one particular thing, or trying not to think about something painful. It feels more physical, or almost practical in the moment—like I suddenly need to do something, stand up, cross the room, get something, adjust something.\n\nIf I’m sitting there and I get up, I might feel a bit relieved afterward, but I wouldn’t say it’s relief from a thought. It’s more like the discomfort of sitting still eases for a moment. But no, I haven’t noticed that it’s tied to avoiding any specific thoughts or emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it can happen when I’m in a perfectly good mood. It’s not really tied to being upset or irritated, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor example, even at dinner when things are pleasant, or in a meeting where I’m not especially stressed, I’ll still suddenly feel like I should get up—refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, something like that. In the moment it feels reasonable, like I’ve got a little task to do, but afterward I can see it wasn’t urgent at all. Everyone else is still seated, and I’m the one who has popped up again.\n\nSo yes, I wouldn’t say it only happens when I’m in a bad mood. It can happen even when I’m feeling fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what others think of you when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do sometimes become aware of how I look to other people if I’m the only one sitting quietly and everyone else seems busy, but I don’t think the main feeling is worry about being judged for sitting still.\n\nIf anything, it’s almost the opposite in some situations—I’m more likely to worry after I’ve moved or stood up again, like, “Oh, are people noticing that I keep doing that?” But when I’m sitting still and not doing anything, I don’t usually have a strong thought like, “They must think I’m lazy,” or “They’re judging me.” It’s not really that sort of social concern.\n\nI suppose as a teacher I’m used to being visible in a room, and I probably am a bit conscious of what’s appropriate in different settings. But no, I haven’t noticed that I’m getting up because I’m afraid of what people think when I’m just sitting there. That doesn’t quite fit, at least not as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you get bored or mentally restless when you're sitting still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think “mentally restless” is probably a good way to put it, although it often shows up physically before I’ve even named it as boredom.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a long time—like at my desk grading essays, especially when there’s a big stack of them—I’ll catch myself tapping my foot under the desk, or clicking my pen, or shifting in the chair again and again. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I’m bored,” exactly. Sometimes I’m interested enough in what I’m doing, or I know it needs doing, but my body just doesn’t seem to want to stay settled.\n\nIt happens in places like church too, where I know perfectly well I’m supposed to be still. I’ll realize I’ve been moving my foot, or changing position, or fiddling with something in my hand, and then I have to sort of tell myself, “Stop that, just sit still.” I can settle down for a bit when I notice it, but then after a while I’ll find I’m doing it again.\n\nSo yes, I do get that restless feeling when I’m sitting still for too long, but it’s a kind of low-level, fidgety restlessness more than some dramatic feeling of boredom.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because you're trying to find something more interesting to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that I’m looking for something more interesting, no. It’s not usually like I’m sitting there thinking, “This is dull, I need to go find something better.”\n\nWhat happens is more automatic than that. In a meeting, for instance, I’ll suddenly feel like I should get up to check a handout, or go to the back of the room, or refill my coffee. Or at dinner, even when everyone else is still seated and the conversation is perfectly fine, I’ll pop up as if there’s some little task that needs doing. In the moment it feels sensible, almost practical, but afterward I’ll realize it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nSo I suppose movement gives me something to do, but I wouldn’t say I’m deliberately searching for something more interesting. It’s more that I don’t seem to stay seated as naturally as other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think stress does make it worse, though it’s not always that I feel “anxious” in a neat, obvious way. It’s more like I can’t stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nSince the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I notice it more in the evenings. I’ll tell myself, “Tonight I’m going to sit down and rest,” and then somehow I’m lesson planning, then I notice laundry needs switching over, then I’m getting things together for softball practice, then I’m chopping food for some community event, and I don’t really pause between any of it. It’s not even always that everything is urgent. It just feels very hard to be still when there’s pressure or a lot on my mind.\n\nSo yes, under stress I do seem to stay in motion more than I mean to. Even on nights when I’ve promised myself I’ll rest, I end up bustling around the house from task to task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to get up and move around might be related to trying to make sure everything is perfect or in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually about making things perfect, no. I mean, I like things to be reasonably organized, but that’s not what it feels like in the moment.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is more that I keep getting up when everyone else is still seated. In meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should check that handout,” or I’ll walk to the back of the room for some reason, or refill my coffee even if I don’t really need more coffee. And at dinner, it’s the same sort of thing—I’ll pop up to do some small thing while other people are still sitting and talking.\n\nAt the time it feels like there’s a reason for it, but when I look back, nothing was actually urgent or out of order. It’s not like I’m straightening every fork or making sure the room is perfect. It’s more just this pull to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness might be related to a fear of something going wrong if you don't keep moving or checking on things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a fear that something terrible will happen, exactly. It doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up and check this, something will go wrong.” At least, that’s not how it sounds in my head.\n\nIt’s more that I just keep finding myself up out of my seat before I’ve really thought it through. Like in meetings, I’ll suddenly decide I need to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or go to the back of the room for some reason. interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: In group discussions I often jump in before someone has finished their thought, especially at description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even as a girl, my report cards said I was bright but could not stay in my seat or wait my turn, and my mother used to joke that I wore a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was ten.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started to affect my relationships at work and in my community because people lose patience with me in meetings and book club, and I leave feeling embarrassed instead of connected.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably harder to manage for about eight months, since my youngest left for college and the house became quieter.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think stress does make it worse, though it’s not always that I feel “anxious” in a neat, obvious way. It’s more like I can’t stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nSince the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I notice it more in the evenings. I’ll tell myself, “Tonight I’m going to sit down and rest,” and then somehow I’m lesson planning, then I notice laundry needs switching over, then I’m getting things together for softball practice, then I’m chopping food for some community event, and I don’t really pause between any of it. It’s not even always that everything is urgent. It just feels very hard to be still when there’s pressure or a lot on my mind.\n\nSo yes, under stress I do seem to stay in motion more than I mean to. Even on nights when I’ve promised myself I’ll rest, I end up bustling around the house from task to task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to get up and move around might be related to trying to make sure everything is perfect or in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually about making things perfect, no. I mean, I like things to be reasonably organized, but that’s not what it feels like in the moment.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is more that I keep getting up when everyone else is still seated. In meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should check that handout,” or I’ll walk to the back of the room for some reason, or refill my coffee even if I don’t really need more coffee. And at dinner, it’s the same sort of thing—I’ll pop up to do some small thing while other people are still sitting and talking.\n\nAt the time it feels like there’s a reason for it, but when I look back, nothing was actually urgent or out of order. It’s not like I’m straightening every fork or making sure the room is perfect. It’s more just this pull to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness might be related to a fear of something going wrong if you don't keep moving or checking on things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a fear that something terrible will happen, exactly. It doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up and check this, something will go wrong.” At least, that’s not how it sounds in my head.\n\nIt’s more that I just keep finding myself up out of my seat before I’ve really thought it through. Like in meetings, I’ll suddenly decide I need to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or go to the back of the room for some reason. And at dinner, when everyone else is still seated and talking, I’ll pop up to do some little thing. In the moment it feels like a normal, practical reason to stand up, but afterward I can see that nothing was actually urgent.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s fear-based checking. It’s more like I have trouble staying seated when other people seem able to just remain there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually feel restless when you're feeling sad or depressed?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure it’s specifically when I’m sad or depressed. I mean, I can feel down sometimes, especially with the house being quieter since my youngest left for college, and in those evenings I do notice I’m still moving around a lot. But I don’t know if the sadness is causing it, exactly.

It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, tonight I’m going to rest,” and then I’m up doing lesson planning, and then I notice the laundry needs to be switched over, and then I’m gathering things for softball practice, and then I’m chopping food for some community event. It’s like I move from one thing to the next without really pausing long enough to ask whether I need to be doing it right then.

So if I’m feeling a little low, I don’t necessarily sit and mope. I still end up bustling around the house. But I’d say the restlessness is just… there, maybe more noticeable when the house is quiet and I meant to slow down.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2, F41.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally made me come in is this habit I’ve noticed where I just can’t seem to stay seated when everyone else is. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I’ve started to feel embarrassed by it.\n\nFor example, in meetings, I’ll suddenly get up to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room for no real reason. And at dinner, when everyone’s still sitting and talking, I’ll pop up to get something from the kitchen or fuss with something nearby, even if it’s not actually urgent. It’s like I tell myself I’m being useful, but honestly, a lot of the time I think I just feel this need to move. I’m a teacher, so I’m used to being on my feet, but it’s starting to stand out in situations where I’m really supposed to just sit still with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you're forced to sit still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s exactly the feeling, though I don’t always recognize it as anxiety in the moment. It’s more like this uncomfortable restlessness, like my body has decided it’s time to do something even if there isn’t really anything that needs doing.\n\nIn meetings, for instance, I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’ll think, “Oh, I should refill my coffee,” or “Maybe I should go check that handout at the back,” and before I’ve really considered whether it’s necessary, I’m already standing up. And it’s the same at dinner sometimes—everyone else is still sitting and talking, and I’ll get up to go to the kitchen or adjust something nearby, even though, honestly, it could wait. I sort of make an excuse for it in my head, like I’m being helpful or practical, but underneath it I think I just feel uncomfortable staying seated for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Hmm… recurring thoughts that I can’t get out of my head? I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no.\n\nI mean, I do think about things, of course—school things, conversations, little practical things I need to do—but I don’t think I have one particular thought that gets stuck and keeps coming back in a way that feels intrusive or impossible to shake. It’s more that my mind may move on to the next thing quickly, but not that I’m trapped with the same thought over and over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this restlessness happens at any particular time of day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear time-of-day pattern, honestly. It’s not like I could say, “Oh, it always happens in the morning,” or “It’s definitely worse after lunch.” I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nIt seems more tied to the situation than the clock, if that makes sense. Like, if I’m expected to sit still for a while—meetings, meals, that sort of thing—that’s when I become aware of it. But whether that’s at ten in the morning or in the evening, I don’t know that it makes a huge difference. I suppose if I’m tired or if something feels especially drawn out, I might notice it more, but I can’t confidently say there’s a particular time of day when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these things, like getting up and moving around, because you feel like you have to, rather than just because you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “have to” is closer to it than “want to,” although it’s not like a clear thought in my head saying, “You must stand up now.” It’s more subtle than that.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep doing this in situations where everyone else is still seated—meetings, dinner, that kind of thing. I’ll suddenly feel like I need to refill my coffee, or check something at the back of the room, or look at a handout, or just move over there for some reason. And in the moment it feels like it makes sense, like, “Well, I may as well do that now,” but if I look back on it, nothing was actually urgent. The coffee could wait. The handout could wait. I didn’t really need to get up right then.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it feels more like an urge or pressure to move than a simple preference. I don’t necessarily want to be the person who keeps standing up when everyone else is settled. In fact, I’d rather not draw attention to myself that way. But I’ll sort of invent a practical reason, or latch onto one, and then I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around helps you avoid any specific thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of, you know, trying to get away from a particular thought or feeling.\n\nI’ve wondered about that, actually, because when you ask it that way, it makes me think, “Am I avoiding something and just not realizing it?” But I can’t point to anything specific. It’s not usually that I’m upset, or anxious about one particular thing, or trying not to think about something painful. It feels more physical, or almost practical in the moment—like I suddenly need to do something, stand up, cross the room, get something, adjust something.\n\nIf I’m sitting there and I get up, I might feel a bit relieved afterward, but I wouldn’t say it’s relief from a thought. It’s more like the discomfort of sitting still eases for a moment. But no, I haven’t noticed that it’s tied to avoiding any specific thoughts or emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it can happen when I’m in a perfectly good mood. It’s not really tied to being upset or irritated, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor example, even at dinner when things are pleasant, or in a meeting where I’m not especially stressed, I’ll still suddenly feel like I should get up—refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, something like that. In the moment it feels reasonable, like I’ve got a little task to do, but afterward I can see it wasn’t urgent at all. Everyone else is still seated, and I’m the one who has popped up again.\n\nSo yes, I wouldn’t say it only happens when I’m in a bad mood. It can happen even when I’m feeling fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what others think of you when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do sometimes become aware of how I look to other people if I’m the only one sitting quietly and everyone else seems busy, but I don’t think the main feeling is worry about being judged for sitting still.\n\nIf anything, it’s almost the opposite in some situations—I’m more likely to worry after I’ve moved or stood up again, like, “Oh, are people noticing that I keep doing that?” But when I’m sitting still and not doing anything, I don’t usually have a strong thought like, “They must think I’m lazy,” or “They’re judging me.” It’s not really that sort of social concern.\n\nI suppose as a teacher I’m used to being visible in a room, and I probably am a bit conscious of what’s appropriate in different settings. But no, I haven’t noticed that I’m getting up because I’m afraid of what people think when I’m just sitting there. That doesn’t quite fit, at least not as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you get bored or mentally restless when you're sitting still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think “mentally restless” is probably a good way to put it, although it often shows up physically before I’ve even named it as boredom.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a long time—like at my desk grading essays, especially when there’s a big stack of them—I’ll catch myself tapping my foot under the desk, or clicking my pen, or shifting in the chair again and again. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I’m bored,” exactly. Sometimes I’m interested enough in what I’m doing, or I know it needs doing, but my body just doesn’t seem to want to stay settled.\n\nIt happens in places like church too, where I know perfectly well I’m supposed to be still. I’ll realize I’ve been moving my foot, or changing position, or fiddling with something in my hand, and then I have to sort of tell myself, “Stop that, just sit still.” I can settle down for a bit when I notice it, but then after a while I’ll find I’m doing it again.\n\nSo yes, I do get that restless feeling when I’m sitting still for too long, but it’s a kind of low-level, fidgety restlessness more than some dramatic feeling of boredom.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because you're trying to find something more interesting to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that I’m looking for something more interesting, no. It’s not usually like I’m sitting there thinking, “This is dull, I need to go find something better.”\n\nWhat happens is more automatic than that. In a meeting, for instance, I’ll suddenly feel like I should get up to check a handout, or go to the back of the room, or refill my coffee. Or at dinner, even when everyone else is still seated and the conversation is perfectly fine, I’ll pop up as if there’s some little task that needs doing. In the moment it feels sensible, almost practical, but afterward I’ll realize it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nSo I suppose movement gives me something to do, but I wouldn’t say I’m deliberately searching for something more interesting. It’s more that I don’t seem to stay seated as naturally as other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think stress does make it worse, though it’s not always that I feel “anxious” in a neat, obvious way. It’s more like I can’t stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nSince the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I notice it more in the evenings. I’ll tell myself, “Tonight I’m going to sit down and rest,” and then somehow I’m lesson planning, then I notice laundry needs switching over, then I’m getting things together for softball practice, then I’m chopping food for some community event, and I don’t really pause between any of it. It’s not even always that everything is urgent. It just feels very hard to be still when there’s pressure or a lot on my mind.\n\nSo yes, under stress I do seem to stay in motion more than I mean to. I sort of make an excuse for it in my head, like I’m being helpful or practical, but underneath it I think I just feel uncomfortable staying seated for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Hmm… recurring thoughts that I can’t get out of my head? It’s more that my mind may move on to the next thing quickly, but not that I’m trapped with the same thought over and over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this restlessness happens at any particular time of day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear time-of-day pattern, honestly. It’s not like I could say, “Oh, it always happens in the morning,” or “It’s definitely worse after lunch.” I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nIt seems more tied to the situation than the clock, if that makes sense. Like, if I’m expected to sit still for a while—meetings, meals, that sort of thing—that’s when I become aware of it. But whether that’s at ten in the morning or in the evening, I don’t know that it makes a huge difference. I suppose if I’m tired or if something feels especially drawn out, I might notice it more, but I can’t confidently say there’s a particular time of day when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these things, like getting up and moving around, because you feel like you have to, rather than just because you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “have to” is closer to it than “want to,” although it’s not like a clear thought in my head saying, “You must stand up now.” It’s more subtle than that.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep doing this in situations where everyone else is still seated—meetings, dinner, that kind of thing. I’ll suddenly feel like I need to refill my coffee, or check something at the back of the room, or look at a handout, or just move over there for some reason. And in the moment it feels like it makes sense, like, “Well, I may as well do that now,” but if I look back on it, nothing was actually urgent. The coffee could wait. The handout could wait. I didn’t really need to get up right then.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it feels more like an urge or pressure to move than a simple preference. I don’t necessarily want to be the person who keeps standing up when everyone else is settled. In fact, I’d rather not draw attention to myself that way. But I’ll sort of invent a practical reason, or latch onto one, and then I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around helps you avoid any specific thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of, you know, trying to get away from a particular thought or feeling.\n\nI’ve wondered about that, actually, because when you ask it that way, it makes me think, “Am I avoiding something and just not realizing it?” But I can’t point to anything specific. It’s not usually that I’m upset, or anxious about one particular thing, or trying not to think about something painful. It feels more physical, or almost practical in the moment—like I suddenly need to do something, stand up, cross the room, get something, adjust something.\n\nIf I’m sitting there and I get up, I might feel a bit relieved afterward, but I wouldn’t say it’s relief from a thought. It’s more like the discomfort of sitting still eases for a moment. But no, I haven’t noticed that it’s tied to avoiding any specific thoughts or emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it can happen when I’m in a perfectly good mood. It’s not really tied to being upset or irritated, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor example, even at dinner when things are pleasant, or in a meeting where I’m not especially stressed, I’ll still suddenly feel like I should get up—refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, something like that. In the moment it feels reasonable, like I’ve got a little task to do, but afterward I can see it wasn’t urgent at all. Everyone else is still seated, and I’m the one who has popped up again.\n\nSo yes, I wouldn’t say it only happens when I’m in a bad mood. It can happen even when I’m feeling fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what others think of you when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do sometimes become aware of how I look to other people if I’m the only one sitting quietly and everyone else seems busy, but I don’t think the main feeling is worry about being judged for sitting still.\n\nIf anything, it’s almost the opposite in some situations—I’m more likely to worry after I’ve moved or stood up again, like, “Oh, are people noticing that I keep doing that?” But when I’m sitting still and not doing anything, I don’t usually have a strong thought like, “They must think I’m lazy,” or “They’re judging me.” It’s not really that sort of social concern.\n\nI suppose as a teacher I’m used to being visible in a room, and I probably am a bit conscious of what’s appropriate in different settings. But no, I haven’t noticed that I’m getting up because I’m afraid of what people think when I’m just sitting there. That doesn’t quite fit, at least not as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you get bored or mentally restless when you're sitting still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think “mentally restless” is probably a good way to put it, although it often shows up physically before I’ve even named it as boredom.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a long time—like at my desk grading essays, especially when there’s a big stack of them—I’ll catch myself tapping my foot under the desk, or clicking my pen, or shifting in the chair again and again. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I’m bored,” exactly. Sometimes I’m interested enough in what I’m doing, or I know it needs doing, but my body just doesn’t seem to want to stay settled.\n\nIt happens in places like church too, where I know perfectly well I’m supposed to be still. I’ll realize I’ve been moving my foot, or changing position, or fiddling with something in my hand, and then I have to sort of tell myself, “Stop that, just sit still.” I can settle down for a bit when I notice it, but then after a while I’ll find I’m doing it again.\n\nSo yes, I do get that restless feeling when I’m sitting still for too long, but it’s a kind of low-level, fidgety restlessness more than some dramatic feeling of boredom.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because you're trying to find something more interesting to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that I’m looking for something more interesting, no. It’s not usually like I’m sitting there thinking, “This is dull, I need to go find something better.”\n\nWhat happens is more automatic than that. In a meeting, for instance, I’ll suddenly feel like I should get up to check a handout, or go to the back of the room, or refill my coffee. Or at dinner, even when everyone else is still seated and the conversation is perfectly fine, I’ll pop up as if there’s some little task that needs doing. In the moment it feels sensible, almost practical, but afterward I’ll realize it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nSo I suppose movement gives me something to do, but I wouldn’t say I’m deliberately searching for something more interesting. It’s more that I don’t seem to stay seated as naturally as other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think stress does make it worse, though it’s not always that I feel “anxious” in a neat, obvious way. It’s more like I can’t stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nSince the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I notice it more in the evenings. I’ll tell myself, “Tonight I’m going to sit down and rest,” and then somehow I’m lesson planning, then I notice laundry needs switching over, then I’m getting things together for softball practice, then I’m chopping food for some community event, and I don’t really pause between any of it. It’s not even always that everything is urgent. It just feels very hard to be still when there’s pressure or a lot on my mind.\n\nSo yes, under stress I do seem to stay in motion more than I mean to. Even on nights when I’ve promised myself I’ll rest, I end up bustling around the house from task to task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to get up and move around might be related to trying to make sure everything is perfect or in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually about making things perfect, no. I mean, I like things to be reasonably organized, but that’s not what it feels like in the moment.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is more that I keep getting up when everyone else is still seated. In meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should check that handout,” or I’ll walk to the back of the room for some reason, or refill my coffee even if I don’t really need more coffee. And at dinner, it’s the same sort of thing—I’ll pop up to do some small thing while other people are still sitting and talking.\n\nAt the time it feels like there’s a reason for it, but when I look back, nothing was actually urgent or out of order. It’s not like I’m straightening every fork or making sure the room is perfect. It’s more just this pull to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness might be related to a fear of something going wrong if you don't keep moving or checking on things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a fear that something terrible will happen, exactly. It doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up and check this, something will go wrong.” At least, that’s not how it sounds in my head.\n\nIt’s more that I just keep finding myself up out of my seat before I’ve really thought it through. Like in meetings, I’ll suddenly decide I need to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or go to the back of the room for some reason. And at dinner, when everyone else is still seated and talking, I’ll pop up to do some little thing. In the moment it feels like a normal, practical reason to stand up, but afterward I can see that nothing was actually urgent.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s fear-based checking. It’s more like I have trouble staying seated when other people seem able to just remain there.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically when I’m sad or depressed. I mean, I can feel down sometimes, especially with the house being quieter since my youngest left for college, and in those evenings I do notice I’m still moving around a lot. But I don’t know if the sadness is causing it, exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, tonight I’m going to rest,” and then I’m up doing lesson planning, and then I notice the laundry needs to be switched over, and then I’m gathering things for softball practice, and then I’m chopping food for some community event. It’s like I move from one thing to the next without really pausing long enough to ask whether I need to be doing it right then.\n\nSo if I’m feeling a little low, I don’t necessarily sit and mope. I still end up bustling around the house. But I’d say the restlessness is just… there, maybe more noticeable when the house is quiet and I meant to slow down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that’s become more obvious lately, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college. I’ll tell myself, very deliberately, “Tonight I’m just going to rest. I’m going to sit down, maybe read or watch something,” and then somehow I’m not doing that at all.

I’ll start with lesson planning, because I’ll think, well, I just need to get one thing ready for school. Then I’m up because the laundry needs to be switched over. Then I remember something for softball practice, so I’m gathering equipment or making a list. Then I’ll be in the kitchen chopping food for some community event or getting things ready for the next day. And it’s not like any one of those things is completely unreasonable, but I move from one to the next without much of a pause.

It’s only later that I realize I never actually relaxed, even though that was the whole plan. I don’t feel very good at just sitting and not doing something.

I sort of make an excuse for it in my head, like I’m being helpful or practical, but underneath it I think I just feel uncomfortable staying seated for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Hmm… recurring thoughts that I can’t get out of my head? I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no.\n\nI mean, I do think about things, of course—school things, conversations, little practical things I need to do—but I don’t think I have one particular thought that gets stuck and keeps coming back in a way that feels intrusive or impossible to shake. It’s more that my mind may move on to the next thing quickly, but not that I’m trapped with the same thought over and over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this restlessness happens at any particular time of day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear time-of-day pattern, honestly. It’s not like I could say, “Oh, it always happens in the morning,” or “It’s definitely worse after lunch.” I haven’t really tracked it that closely.\n\nIt seems more tied to the situation than the clock, if that makes sense. Like, if I’m expected to sit still for a while—meetings, meals, that sort of thing—that’s when I become aware of it. But whether that’s at ten in the morning or in the evening, I don’t know that it makes a huge difference. I suppose if I’m tired or if something feels especially drawn out, I might notice it more, but I can’t confidently say there’s a particular time of day when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these things, like getting up and moving around, because you feel like you have to, rather than just because you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “have to” is closer to it than “want to,” although it’s not like a clear thought in my head saying, “You must stand up now.” It’s more subtle than that.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep doing this in situations where everyone else is still seated—meetings, dinner, that kind of thing. I’ll suddenly feel like I need to refill my coffee, or check something at the back of the room, or look at a handout, or just move over there for some reason. And in the moment it feels like it makes sense, like, “Well, I may as well do that now,” but if I look back on it, nothing was actually urgent. The coffee could wait. The handout could wait. I didn’t really need to get up right then.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it feels more like an urge or pressure to move than a simple preference. I don’t necessarily want to be the person who keeps standing up when everyone else is settled. In fact, I’d rather not draw attention to myself that way. But I’ll sort of invent a practical reason, or latch onto one, and then I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around helps you avoid any specific thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of, you know, trying to get away from a particular thought or feeling.\n\nI’ve wondered about that, actually, because when you ask it that way, it makes me think, “Am I avoiding something and just not realizing it?” But I can’t point to anything specific. It’s not usually that I’m upset, or anxious about one particular thing, or trying not to think about something painful. It feels more physical, or almost practical in the moment—like I suddenly need to do something, stand up, cross the room, get something, adjust something.\n\nIf I’m sitting there and I get up, I might feel a bit relieved afterward, but I wouldn’t say it’s relief from a thought. It’s more like the discomfort of sitting still eases for a moment. But no, I haven’t noticed that it’s tied to avoiding any specific thoughts or emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it can happen when I’m in a perfectly good mood. It’s not really tied to being upset or irritated, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor example, even at dinner when things are pleasant, or in a meeting where I’m not especially stressed, I’ll still suddenly feel like I should get up—refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, something like that. In the moment it feels reasonable, like I’ve got a little task to do, but afterward I can see it wasn’t urgent at all. Everyone else is still seated, and I’m the one who has popped up again.\n\nSo yes, I wouldn’t say it only happens when I’m in a bad mood. It can happen even when I’m feeling fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what others think of you when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do sometimes become aware of how I look to other people if I’m the only one sitting quietly and everyone else seems busy, but I don’t think the main feeling is worry about being judged for sitting still.\n\nIf anything, it’s almost the opposite in some situations—I’m more likely to worry after I’ve moved or stood up again, like, “Oh, are people noticing that I keep doing that?” But when I’m sitting still and not doing anything, I don’t usually have a strong thought like, “They must think I’m lazy,” or “They’re judging me.” It’s not really that sort of social concern.\n\nI suppose as a teacher I’m used to being visible in a room, and I probably am a bit conscious of what’s appropriate in different settings. But no, I haven’t noticed that I’m getting up because I’m afraid of what people think when I’m just sitting there. That doesn’t quite fit, at least not as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you get bored or mentally restless when you're sitting still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think “mentally restless” is probably a good way to put it, although it often shows up physically before I’ve even named it as boredom.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a long time—like at my desk grading essays, especially when there’s a big stack of them—I’ll catch myself tapping my foot under the desk, or clicking my pen, or shifting in the chair again and again. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I’m bored,” exactly. Sometimes I’m interested enough in what I’m doing, or I know it needs doing, but my body just doesn’t seem to want to stay settled.\n\nIt happens in places like church too, where I know perfectly well I’m supposed to be still. I’ll realize I’ve been moving my foot, or changing position, or fiddling with something in my hand, and then I have to sort of tell myself, “Stop that, just sit still.” I can settle down for a bit when I notice it, but then after a while I’ll find I’m doing it again.\n\nSo yes, I do get that restless feeling when I’m sitting still for too long, but it’s a kind of low-level, fidgety restlessness more than some dramatic feeling of boredom.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because you're trying to find something more interesting to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that I’m looking for something more interesting, no. It’s not usually like I’m sitting there thinking, “This is dull, I need to go find something better.”\n\nWhat happens is more automatic than that. In a meeting, for instance, I’ll suddenly feel like I should get up to check a handout, or go to the back of the room, or refill my coffee. Or at dinner, even when everyone else is still seated and the conversation is perfectly fine, I’ll pop up as if there’s some little task that needs doing. In the moment it feels sensible, almost practical, but afterward I’ll realize it wasn’t urgent at all.\n\nSo I suppose movement gives me something to do, but I wouldn’t say I’m deliberately searching for something more interesting. It’s more that I don’t seem to stay seated as naturally as other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think stress does make it worse, though it’s not always that I feel “anxious” in a neat, obvious way. It’s more like I can’t stop moving from one thing to the next.\n\nSince the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I notice it more in the evenings. I’ll tell myself, “Tonight I’m going to sit down and rest,” and then somehow I’m lesson planning, then I notice laundry needs switching over, then I’m getting things together for softball practice, then I’m chopping food for some community event, and I don’t really pause between any of it. It’s not even always that everything is urgent. It just feels very hard to be still when there’s pressure or a lot on my mind.\n\nSo yes, under stress I do seem to stay in motion more than I mean to. Even on nights when I’ve promised myself I’ll rest, I end up bustling around the house from task to task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to get up and move around might be related to trying to make sure everything is perfect or in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually about making things perfect, no. I mean, I like things to be reasonably organized, but that’s not what it feels like in the moment.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is more that I keep getting up when everyone else is still seated. In meetings I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should check that handout,” or I’ll walk to the back of the room for some reason, or refill my coffee even if I don’t really need more coffee. And at dinner, it’s the same sort of thing—I’ll pop up to do some small thing while other people are still sitting and talking.\n\nAt the time it feels like there’s a reason for it, but when I look back, nothing was actually urgent or out of order. It’s not like I’m straightening every fork or making sure the room is perfect. It’s more just this pull to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness might be related to a fear of something going wrong if you don't keep moving or checking on things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a fear that something terrible will happen, exactly. It doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up and check this, something will go wrong.” At least, that’s not how it sounds in my head.\n\nIt’s more that I just keep finding myself up out of my seat before I’ve really thought it through. Like in meetings, I’ll suddenly decide I need to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or go to the back of the room for some reason. And at dinner, when everyone else is still seated and talking, I’ll pop up to do some little thing. In the moment it feels like a normal, practical reason to stand up, but afterward I can see that nothing was actually urgent.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s fear-based checking. It’s more like I have trouble staying seated when other people seem able to just remain there.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically when I’m sad or depressed. I mean, I can feel down sometimes, especially with the house being quieter since my youngest left for college, and in those evenings I do notice I’m still moving around a lot. But I don’t know if the sadness is causing it, exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, tonight I’m going to rest,” and then I’m up doing lesson planning, and then I notice the laundry needs to be switched over, and then I’m gathering things for softball practice, and then I’m chopping food for some community event. It’s like I move from one thing to the next without really pausing long enough to ask whether I need to be doing it right then.\n\nSo if I’m feeling a little low, I don’t necessarily sit and mope. I still end up bustling around the house. But I’d say the restlessness is just… there, maybe more noticeable when the house is quiet and I meant to slow down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble relaxing when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that’s become more obvious lately, since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college. I’ll tell myself, very deliberately, “Tonight I’m just going to rest. I’m going to sit down, maybe read or watch something,” and then somehow I’m not doing that at all.\n\nI’ll start with lesson planning, because I’ll think, well, I just need to get one thing ready for school. Then I’m up because the laundry needs to be switched over. Then I remember something for softball practice, so I’m gathering equipment or making a list. Then I’ll be in the kitchen chopping food for some community event or getting things ready for the next day. And it’s not like any one of those things is completely unreasonable, but I move from one to the next without much of a pause.\n\nIt’s only later that I realize I never actually relaxed, even though that was the whole plan. I don’t feel very good at just sitting and not doing something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s like I move from one thing to the next without really pausing long enough to ask whether I need to be doing it right then.\n\nSo if I’m feeling a little low, I don’t necessarily sit and mope. I still end up bustling around the house. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. When I’m sitting still, I don’t usually notice myself going into long worries about the past or future, like replaying old mistakes or imagining disasters ahead. I mean, I might think, “Oh, I should remember to do that tomorrow,” or “I wonder if I handled that conversation well,” but it doesn’t feel like I’m sitting there consumed by worry.

It’s more that sitting still itself feels… difficult to maintain. Sometimes my mind will just jump to some practical thing, and then before I’ve really examined whether it matters, I’m up doing it. But I don’t think there’s a big anxious story underneath it most of the time. At least not that I’ve noticed.

I suppose if you asked my husband, he might say I always seem like I’m thinking of the next thing, but inside it doesn’t feel like worrying so much as just… my attention moving on and my body following it.

And at dinner, when everyone’s still sitting and talking, I’ll pop up to get something from the kitchen or fuss with something nearby, even if it’s not actually urgent. It’s like I tell myself I’m being useful, but honestly, a lot of the time I think I just feel this need to move. I’m a teacher, so I’m used to being on my feet, but it’s starting to stand out in situations where I’m really supposed to just sit still with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you're forced to sit still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s exactly the feeling, though I don’t always recognize it as anxiety in the moment. It’s more like this uncomfortable restlessness, like my body has decided it’s time to do something even if there isn’t really anything that needs doing.\n\nIn meetings, for instance, I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’ll think, “Oh, I should refill my coffee,” or “Maybe I should go check that handout at the back,” and before I’ve really considered whether it’s necessary, I’m already standing up. And it’s the same at dinner sometimes—everyone else is still sitting and talking, and I’ll get up to go to the kitchen or adjust something nearby, even though, honestly, it could wait. I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no.\n\nI mean, I do think about things, of course—school things, conversations, little practical things I need to do—but I don’t think I have one particular thought that gets stuck and keeps coming back in a way that feels intrusive or impossible to shake. It’s more that my mind may move on to the next thing quickly, but not that I’m trapped with the same thought over and over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this restlessness happens at any particular time of day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear time-of-day pattern, honestly. Like, if I’m expected to sit still for a while—meetings, meals, that sort of thing—that’s when I become aware of it. But whether that’s at ten in the morning or in the evening, I don’t know that it makes a huge difference. I suppose if I’m tired or if something feels especially drawn out, I might notice it more, but I can’t confidently say there’s a particular time of day when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these things, like getting up and moving around, because you feel like you have to, rather than just because you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “have to” is closer to it than “want to,” although it’s not like a clear thought in my head saying, “You must stand up now.” It’s more subtle than that.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I keep doing this in situations where everyone else is still seated—meetings, dinner, that kind of thing. I’ll suddenly feel like I need to refill my coffee, or check something at the back of the room, or look at a handout, or just move over there for some reason. And in the moment it feels like it makes sense, like, “Well, I may as well do that now,” but if I look back on it, nothing was actually urgent. The coffee could wait. The handout could wait. I didn’t really need to get up right then.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it feels more like an urge or pressure to move than a simple preference. I don’t necessarily want to be the person who keeps standing up when everyone else is settled. In fact, I’d rather not draw attention to myself that way. But I’ll sort of invent a practical reason, or latch onto one, and then I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around helps you avoid any specific thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of, you know, trying to get away from a particular thought or feeling.\n\nI’ve wondered about that, actually, because when you ask it that way, it makes me think, “Am I avoiding something and just not realizing it?” But I can’t point to anything specific. It’s not usually that I’m upset, or anxious about one particular thing, or trying not to think about something painful. It feels more physical, or almost practical in the moment—like I suddenly need to do something, stand up, cross the room, get something, adjust something.\n\nIf I’m sitting there and I get up, I might feel a bit relieved afterward, but I wouldn’t say it’s relief from a thought. It’s more like the discomfort of sitting still eases for a moment. But no, I haven’t noticed that it’s tied to avoiding any specific thoughts or emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it can happen when I’m in a perfectly good mood. It’s not really tied to being upset or irritated, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor example, even at dinner when things are pleasant, or in a meeting where I’m not especially stressed, I’ll still suddenly feel like I should get up—refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, something like that. In the moment it feels reasonable, like I’ve got a little task to do, but afterward I can see it wasn’t urgent at all. Everyone else is still seated, and I’m the one who has popped up again.\n\nSo yes, I wouldn’t say it only happens when I’m in a bad mood. It can happen even when I’m feeling fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what others think of you when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do sometimes become aware of how I look to other people if I’m the only one sitting quietly and everyone else seems busy, but I don’t think the main feeling is worry about being judged for sitting still.\n\nIf anything, it’s almost the opposite in some situations—I’m more likely to worry after I’ve moved or stood up again, like, “Oh, are people noticing that I keep doing that?” But when I’m sitting still and not doing anything, I don’t usually have a strong thought like, “They must think I’m lazy,” or “They’re judging me.” It’s not really that sort of social concern.\n\nI suppose as a teacher I’m used to being visible in a room, and I probably am a bit conscious of what’s appropriate in different settings. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I’ve started to feel embarrassed by it.\n\nFor example, in meetings, I’ll suddenly get up to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room for no real reason. And at dinner, when everyone’s still sitting and talking, I’ll pop up to get something from the kitchen or fuss with something nearby, even if it’s not actually urgent. It’s like I tell myself I’m being useful, but honestly, a lot of the time I think I just feel this need to move. I’m a teacher, so I’m used to being on my feet, but it’s starting to stand out in situations where I’m really supposed to just sit still with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you're forced to sit still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s exactly the feeling, though I don’t always recognize it as anxiety in the moment. I don’t necessarily want to be the person who keeps standing up when everyone else is settled. In fact, I’d rather not draw attention to myself that way. It’s not usually that I’m upset, or anxious about one particular thing, or trying not to think about something painful. It feels more physical, or almost practical in the moment—like I suddenly need to do something, stand up, cross the room, get something, adjust something.\n\nIf I’m sitting there and I get up, I might feel a bit relieved afterward, but I wouldn’t say it’s relief from a thought. It’s more like the discomfort of sitting still eases for a moment. But no, I haven’t noticed that it’s tied to avoiding any specific thoughts or emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it can happen when I’m in a perfectly good mood. It’s not really tied to being upset or irritated, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor example, even at dinner when things are pleasant, or in a meeting where I’m not especially stressed, I’ll still suddenly feel like I should get up—refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, something like that. In the moment it feels reasonable, like I’ve got a little task to do, but afterward I can see it wasn’t urgent at all. Everyone else is still seated, and I’m the one who has popped up again.\n\nSo yes, I wouldn’t say it only happens when I’m in a bad mood. It can happen even when I’m feeling fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about what others think of you when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I do sometimes become aware of how I look to other people if I’m the only one sitting quietly and everyone else seems busy, but I don’t think the main feeling is worry about being judged for sitting still.\n\nIf anything, it’s almost the opposite in some situations—I’m more likely to worry after I’ve moved or stood up again, like, “Oh, are people noticing that I keep doing that?” But when I’m sitting still and not doing anything, I don’t usually have a strong thought like, “They must think I’m lazy,” or “They’re judging me.” It’s not really that sort of social concern.\n\nI suppose as a teacher I’m used to being visible in a room, and I probably am a bit conscious of what’s appropriate in different settings. But no, I haven’t noticed that I’m getting up because I’m afraid of what people think when I’m just sitting there. That doesn’t quite fit, at least not as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you get bored or mentally restless when you're sitting still for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think “mentally restless” is probably a good way to put it, although it often shows up physically before I’ve even named it as boredom.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a long time—like at my desk grading essays, especially when there’s a big stack of them—I’ll catch myself tapping my foot under the desk, or clicking my pen, or shifting in the chair again and again. It’s not always that I’m thinking, “I’m bored,” exactly. Sometimes I’m interested enough in what I’m doing, or I know it needs doing, but my body just doesn’t seem to want to stay settled.\n\nIt happens in places like church too, where I know perfectly well I’m supposed to be still. I’ll realize I’ve been moving my foot, or changing position, or fiddling with something in my hand, and then I have to sort of tell myself, “Stop that, just sit still.” I can settle down for a bit when I notice it, but then after a while I’ll find I’m doing it again.\n\nSo yes, I do get that restless feeling when I’m sitting still for too long, but it’s a kind of low-level, fidgety restlessness more than some dramatic feeling of boredom.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because you're trying to find something more interesting to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that I’m looking for something more interesting, no. I’ll tell myself, “Tonight I’m going to sit down and rest,” and then somehow I’m lesson planning, then I notice laundry needs switching over, then I’m getting things together for softball practice, then I’m chopping food for some community event, and I don’t really pause between any of it. It’s not even always that everything is urgent. It just feels very hard to be still when there’s pressure or a lot on my mind.\n\nSo yes, under stress I do seem to stay in motion more than I mean to. Even on nights when I’ve promised myself I’ll rest, I end up bustling around the house from task to task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to get up and move around might be related to trying to make sure everything is perfect or in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually about making things perfect, no. It’s not like I’m straightening every fork or making sure the room is perfect. It’s more just this pull to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness might be related to a fear of something going wrong if you don't keep moving or checking on things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a fear that something terrible will happen, exactly. It doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up and check this, something will go wrong.” At least, that’s not how it sounds in my head.\n\nIt’s more that I just keep finding myself up out of my seat before I’ve really thought it through. Like in meetings, I’ll suddenly decide I need to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or go to the back of the room for some reason. And at dinner, when everyone else is still seated and talking, I’ll pop up to do some little thing. In the moment it feels like a normal, practical reason to stand up, but afterward I can see that nothing was actually urgent.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s fear-based checking. It’s more like I have trouble staying seated when other people seem able to just remain there.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s specifically when I’m sad or depressed. I mean, I can feel down sometimes, especially with the house being quieter since my youngest left for college, and in those evenings I do notice I’m still moving around a lot. But I don’t know if the sadness is causing it, exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, tonight I’m going to rest,” and then I’m up doing lesson planning, and then I notice the laundry needs to be switched over, and then I’m gathering things for softball practice, and then I’m chopping food for some community event. It’s like I move from one thing to the next without really pausing long enough to ask whether I need to be doing it right then.\n\nSo if I’m feeling a little low, I don’t necessarily sit and mope. I still end up bustling around the house. But I’d say the restlessness is just… there, maybe more noticeable when the house is quiet and I meant to slow down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble relaxing when you're not actively doing something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s usually not one big thought, like, “I have to move now.” It’s more these little practical flashes that pop into my head and feel like they need to be acted on right away, even when they probably don’t.

Like in a meeting, I’ll suddenly think, “Oh, I should check whether that handout is in the right stack,” or “Maybe I should go refill the coffee,” or I’ll notice something at the back of the room and feel like I should just go take care of it. And then I’m standing up before I’ve really decided whether it’s necessary.

It happens at dinner, too, which is a little embarrassing because everyone else is still seated and I’m the one popping up to get more water, or clear something, or check on something in the kitchen. In my mind it feels like I’m being useful, but if I’m honest, nothing is usually urgent. It’s more like the thought gives me a reason to get up. That’s actually part of what made me come in, because I started noticing how often I’m the person who leaves my seat when everyone else is just… sitting.
